Classification of Quantum Graphs on M 2 M_{2} and their Quantum Automorphism Groups
Junichiro Matsuda

Abstract
Motivated by string diagrammatic approach to undirected tracial quantum graphs by Musto, Reutter, Verdon [ 13 ] , in the former part of this paper we diagrammatically formulate directed nontracial quantum graphs by Brannan, Chirvasitu, Eifler, Harris, Paulsen, Su, Wasilewski [ 7 ] . In the latter part, we supply a concrete classification of undirected reflexive quantum graphs on M 2 M_{2} and their quantum automorphism groups in both tracial and nontracial settings. We also obtain quantum isomorphisms between tracial quantum graphs on M 2 M_{2} and certain classical graphs, which reproves the monoidal equivalences between S ​ O ​ ( 3 ) SO(3) and S 4 + S_{4}^{+} , and O ⁡ ( 2 ) O(2) and H 2 + H_{2}^{+} .



1
Introduction

The notion of quantum graphs (called noncommutative graphs in
[
10
]
) was first introduced by Duan, Severini, Winter
[
10
]
in terms of operator systems as the confusability graph of a quantum channel in quantum information theory.
As an analogue of the fact that simple undirected classical graphs are irreflexive symmetric relations, Weaver
[
19
]
formulated quantum graphs as reflexive symmetric quantum relations on a von Neumann algebra, which extends
[
10
]
, and quantum relations were introduced by Kuperberg, Weaver
[
12
]
.
Following those works, Musto, Reutter, Verdon
[
13
]
formulated finite quantum graphs as adjacency operators on tracial finite quantum sets,
and Brannan et al.
[
7
]
generalized them for nontracial settings.

The key tool of
[
13
]
are string diagrams formulated by Vicary
[
17
]
, but it should be treated with care if applied to nontracial quantum graphs in
[
7
]
.
So in the former part of this paper, we discuss the diagrammatic formulation of nontracial quantum graphs.

Brannan et al.
[
7
]
also introduced the quantum automorphism groups and bigalois extensions of quantum graphs in order to refine the notion of quantum isomorphisms between quantum graphs.
The quantum automorphism group of classical graphs was first introduced by
Bichon
[
6
, Definition 3.1]
in a slightly different way from
[
7
]
. The origin of the formulation in
[
7
]
is due to Banica
[
4
, Definition 3.2]
, following the quantum symmetry group of finite spaces introduced by Wang
[
18
, Definition 2.3]
.

Although some abstract constructions of a quantum graph from others are given categorically by Musto, Reutter, Verdon
[
14
]
and algebraically by Brannan, Eifler, Voigt, Weber
[
8
]
, few nontrivial concrete examples of them were known.
This motivated the author to compute and classify undirected reflexive quantum graphs and their quantum automorphism groups on the most basic noncommutative algebra
M_{2}
as a first step.

This research was concluded during the MSc studies of the author and completed during the first term of his doctoral study.
After completing the results obtained in this paper, he found that Gromada
[
11
]
independently studied partially the same topic.
Gromada classified undirected tracial quantum graphs on
M_{2}

[
11
, section 3.3]
in an insightful way using Lie algebras and the correspondence between the adjacency operators on tracial
M_{2}
and projections in
M_{2}\otimes M_{2}^{op}
.

1.1
Presentation of results

In section 2, we review basic properties of quantum graphs and generalize the string diagrammatic formulation in
[
13
]
to nontracial cases. An important difference is that the tracial cases allow topological deformation of diagrams while the nontracial cases do not allow deformation through a cusp.
We compare several properties of directed quantum graphs, in particular, an equivalence between reality and complete positivity of quantum graphs is proved.
As a straightforward generalization of
[
13
, Proposition 5.19]
, we show that the category of quantum automorphisms of a quantum graph is isomorphic to the finite-dimensional representation category of the quantum automorphism group algebra of the quantum graph.
We also introduce the regularity of quantum graphs, which helps the classification in the following sections.

In section 3, we directly compute the reflexive undirected quantum graphs on
M_{2}
and classify them up to quantum and classical isomorphisms.
In the tracial case, they are regular and classified by their degree
d\in\{1,2,3,4\}
. In the nontracial case, they are not always regular but still have a similar form.

In section 4, we identify the quantum automorphism groups of the quantum graphs on
M_{2}
classified in section 3. In the tracial case,
SO(3)
and
O(2)
appear as quantum automorphism groups. In the nontracial case, the quantum special orthogonal groups
SO_{q}(3)
and the unitary torus
\mathbb{T}=U(1)
appear.

Observing the spectra, the regular tracial quantum graphs on
M_{2}
are isospectral to regular classical graphs on four vertices, which implies the possibility of quantum isomorphisms between them.
Therefore we compute the bigalois extension, the universal coefficient algebra of quantum isomorphisms between quantum graphs introduced by
[
7
, Definition 4.1]
, to find that they are indeed quantum isomorphic.
Since a quantum isomorphism of quantum graphs induces a monoidal equivalence of their quantum automorphism groups by
[
7
, Theorem 4.7]
,
it follows that
SO(3)
and
S_{4}^{+}
,
O(2)
and
H_{2}^{+}
are monoidally equivalent respectively.
Although this is already known in quantum group theory
[
2
,
5
]
, it exhibits a new approach to monoidal equivalence using quantum graph theory.

Gromada
[
11
, Proposition 8.1]
also obtains the same quantum isomorphisms and monoidal equivalence differently using quantum Cayley graphs as a twist of classical Cayley graphs.

2
Basics in quantum graphs

Throughout this paper, we denote by
(\cdot)^{*}
the involution in a
*
-algebra, and by
(\cdot)^{\dagger}
the adjoint of an operator between Hilbert spaces.

2.1
Quantum sets

Let
B
be a finite dimensional unital
C^{*}
-algebra.

B
is equipped with the bilinear multiplication map

B\times B\ni(a,b)\mapsto ab\in B
,
which induces a linear multiplication operator

m:B\otimes B\ni a\otimes b\mapsto ab\in B

by the universality of tensor product.
We identify
x\in B
with a linear map
\mathbb{C}\ni 1\mapsto x\in B
,
in particular
1
denotes the multiplicative unit in
B
and the unital
*
-homomorphism
\mathbb{C}\hookrightarrow B
.

Let
\psi
be a faithful state on
B
,
then
\langle x,y\rangle_{\psi}=\braket{y|x}_{\psi}=\psi(y^{*}x)

denotes the inner product on
L^{2}(B,\psi)=B
.
The subscript
\psi
of the inner product is often abbreviated if there is no concern of confusion.
Via the Hilbert adjoint with respect to
\braket{\cdot|\cdot}_{\psi}
,
x\in B
induces

x^{\dagger}=\bra{x}=\psi(x^{*}\cdot):B\to\mathbb{C}
,
and the algebra
(B,m,1)
, a vector space
B
equipped with the multiplication
m
and the unit
1
, satisfying

\begin{array}[]{c}\textrm{associativity}\\
(x\ y)\ z=x\ (y\ z)\mathrlap{\ \forall x,y,z\in B}\\
m(m\otimes\mathrm{id})=m(\mathrm{id}\otimes m)\end{array}\qquad\quad\begin{array}[]{c}\textrm{existence of a unit}\\
1\ x=x=x\ 1\mathrlap{\ \forall x\in B}\\
m(1\otimes\mathrm{id})=\mathrm{id}=m(\mathrm{id}\otimes 1)\end{array}

induces a coalgebra
(B,m^{\dagger},\psi)
, a vector space
B
equipped with the comultiplication
m^{\dagger}
and the counit
\psi=1^{\dagger}
, satisfying

\begin{array}[]{c}\textrm{coassociativity}\\
(m^{\dagger}\otimes\mathrm{id})m^{\dagger}=(\mathrm{id}\otimes m^{\dagger})m^{\dagger}\end{array}\quad\begin{array}[]{c}\textrm{existence of a counit}\\
(\psi\otimes\mathrm{id})m^{\dagger}=\mathrm{id}=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\psi)m^{\dagger}\end{array}.

Following Vicary
[
17
]
, we adopt the string diagram notation of operators, which enables our visual understanding and topological calculation.
For operators
f:H_{0}\to H_{1}
and
g:H_{1}\to H_{2}
between Hilbert spaces,
we associate Hilbert spaces with strings, operators with nodes, and read diagrams from bottom to top:

\{v_{i}\}_{i}
is an orthonormal basis (ONB) for
H
,
and
v^{\dagger}=\bra{v}=\braket{v|\cdot}\in H^{*}
for
v=\ket{v}\in H
.
Note that we can naturally identify
H\otimes H^{*}
with
B(H)
by

H\otimes H^{*}\ni\ket{v}\otimes\bra{w}\mapsto\ket{v}\bra{w}\in B(H)
.
Then (
2.1
) is identified with the unit map
\mathbb{C}\ni 1\to\mathrm{id}_{H}\in B(H)

and the canonical trace
\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits:B(H)\to\mathbb{C}
.

The operators (
2.1
) satisfies the following equalities, the so-called snake equation in
[
13
, section 2.2, (5)]
:

For simplicity we denote
\psi m
and

The quintuple
(B,m,1,m^{\dagger},\psi)
forms a Frobenius algebra:

Definition 2.1

(cf. Vicary
[

.

Note that we may compute string diagrams by topological deformation via Frobenius equality, snake equality, associativity, and coassociativity.

Definition 2.2

(Banica
[
3
, section 1]
, Musto, Reutter, Verdon
[
13
, Terminology 3.1]
,
Brannan, et al.
[
7
, Definition 3.1]
)
.

Let
\psi
be a faithful state on a finite dimensional
C^{*}
-algebra
B
as above, and
\delta>0
.
The state
\psi
is called a
\delta
-form
on
B
if the following equality
(so-called
special
in Vicary
[
17
]
) is satisfied:

{{}{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 19.69 M 0 -39.37 L 0 -59.06}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{array}=\delta^{2}\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox to0.4pt{\vbox to57.31pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 78.74}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{array}\textrm{ , i.e., }mm^{\dagger}=\delta^{2}\mathrm{id}_{B}.

And then we call
(B,\psi)
a quantum set.

A quantum set
(B,\psi)
is said to be
commutative
or
symmetric
(tracial) if
B
is commutative or
\psi
is tracial respectively, which are formulated in diagrams as below.

We often use
\tau
instead of
\psi
in the tracial case.

Remark 2.3
.

The notion of
\delta
-form was introduced by Banica
[
3
]
, and Musto et al.
[
13
]
defined quantum sets in the case where
\psi
is a trace. Finally, Brannan, et al.
[
7
]
defined quantum sets as above.
The definition in
[
13
]
is
mm^{\dagger}=\mathrm{id}_{B}
,
which does not have
\delta^{2}
. This is because the counit is normalized as
\psi(1)=\delta^{2}={\left\lvert{B}\right\rvert}
in
[
13
]
, whence
m^{\dagger}
in
[
13
]
is our
m^{\dagger}/\delta^{2}
. Thus these formulations are equivalent.

Lemma 2.4
.

A finite set with the uniform probability measure corresponds to a commutative quantum set via Gelfand duality. In particular
\tau=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits/n
is a
\delta=\sqrt{n}
-form on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
.

Proof.

Let
X=\{1,...,n\}
be an
n
-element set with the uniform probability measure
\mu
.
The pair
(X,\mu)
corresponds to the commutative
C^{*}
-algebra
(C(X),\int\cdot d\mu)
of (continuous) functions on
X
with a tracial state
\int\cdot d\mu
via Gelfand duality.
Moreover
(C(X),\int\cdot d\mu)
is isomorphic to the
n\times n
diagonal matrix algebra
(\mathbb{C}^{n},\tau=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits/n)
with normalized trace
via
C(X)\ni\delta_{i}\mapsto e_{i}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
where
\delta_{i}
is the indicator function of
\{i\}\subseteq X
and
e_{i}
is the matrix unit of
(i,i)
entry.
Since the multiplication
m
is given by
e_{i}\otimes e_{j}\mapsto\delta_{ij}e_{i}
,
the comultiplication
m^{\dagger}
is given by
e_{i}\mapsto ne_{i}\otimes e_{i}
because

\displaystyle\braket{e_{j}\otimes e_{k}|m^{\dagger}e_{i}}_{\tau\otimes\tau}

\displaystyle=\braket{m(e_{j}\otimes e_{k})|e_{i}}_{\tau}=\tau((e_{j}e_{k})^{*}e_{i})=\frac{1}{n}\delta_{ji}\delta_{ki}

\displaystyle=n\braket{e_{j}|e_{i}}_{\tau}\braket{e_{k}|e_{i}}_{\tau}=\braket{e_{j}\otimes e_{k}|ne_{i}\otimes e_{i}}_{\tau\otimes\tau}.

Thus
mm^{\dagger}e_{i}=m(ne_{i}\otimes e_{i})=ne_{i}
, i.e.,

mm^{\dagger}=n\ \mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}
.
Therefore
\tau=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits/n
is a
\delta=\sqrt{n}
-form on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
∎

Although a general quantum set
(B,\psi)
is not symmetric,
it satisfies the following equality,
so-called

{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}}{{{}}{{}}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}}{{{}}{{}}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 0 17.23 36.92 39.37 19.69 39.37 C 2.45 39.37 39.37 17.23 39.37 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{array}.

Thus topological deformations through a cusp are not allowed in nontracial cases, while they are allowed in the tracial case.

Put
B=\bigoplus_{s}M_{n_{s}}
and

\psi=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits(Q\ \cdot)=\bigoplus_{s}\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits_{s}(Q_{s}\ \cdot)
,
where
\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits=\bigoplus_{s}\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits_{s}
is the canonical unnormalized trace given by the sum of diagonal entries or eigenvalues, and
Q=\bigoplus_{s}Q_{s}\in B
.
Note that
Q
is positive definite and
\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits(Q)=\sum_{s}\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits_{s}(Q_{s})=1
if and only if
\psi
is a faithful state.
Since positive matrices are unitarily diagonalizable, we may assume that
Q
is diagonal.

Let
e_{ij,s}
be the matrix unit of
(i,j)
entry of
s
-th direct summand
M_{n_{s}}\subseteq B
,
i.e., the matrix with entries
0
except for
(i,j)
entry
1
of
s
-th direct summand.

Lemma 2.5
.

\{\widetilde{e_{ij,s}}\coloneqq e_{ij,s}Q_{s}^{-1/2}\mid i,j\leq n_{s},s\}
forms an ONB for
L^{2}(B,\psi)
.

Proof.

Since
\{e_{ij,s}\mid i,j\leq n_{s},s\}
forms an ONB for
L^{2}(B,\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits)
,
we have

\braket{e_{kl,r}Q_{r}^{-1/2}|e_{ij,s}Q_{s}^{-1/2}}_{\psi}=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits(Q(e_{kl,r}Q_{r}^{-1/2})^{*}e_{ij,s}Q_{s}^{-1/2})=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits(e_{kl,r}^{*}e_{ij,s})=\delta_{ij,s}^{kl,r}

where
\delta_{ij,s}^{kl,r}\coloneqq\begin{cases}1&\textrm{if }(i,j,s)=(k,l,r)\\
0&\textrm{otherwise}\end{cases}
.
∎

We sometimes describe operators with respect to the basis

\{\widetilde{e_{ij,s}}=e_{ij,s}Q_{s}^{-1/2}\}_{ijs}
as indicated below.

Lemma 2.6
.

•

1=\sum_{ijs}(Q_{s}^{1/2})_{ij}\widetilde{e_{ij,s}}
,

•

\psi:\widetilde{e_{ij,s}}\mapsto(Q_{s}^{1/2})_{ji}
.

•

m:\widetilde{e_{ij,s}}\otimes\widetilde{e_{kl,r}}\mapsto\delta_{rs}(Q_{s}^{-1/2})_{jk}\widetilde{e_{il,r}}
.

•

m^{\dagger}:\widetilde{e_{ij,s}}\mapsto\sum_{u,v\leq n_{s}}(Q_{s}^{-1/2})_{vu}\widetilde{e_{iu,s}}\otimes\widetilde{e_{vj,s}}
.

Proof.

Simple computations show
\braket{\widetilde{e_{ij,s}}|1}=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits(Q_{s}^{1/2}e_{ji,s})=(Q_{s}^{1/2})_{ij}
,

\psi(\widetilde{e_{ij,s}})=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits(Q_{s}^{1/2}e_{ij,s})=(Q_{s}^{1/2})_{ji}
,

\widetilde{e_{ij,s}}\widetilde{e_{kl,r}}=e_{ij,s}Q_{s}^{-1/2}e_{kl,r}Q_{r}^{-1/2}=\delta_{rs}(Q_{s}^{-1/2})_{jk}\widetilde{e_{il,r}}
,
and

\displaystyle\braket{\widetilde{e_{ku,r}}\otimes\widetilde{e_{vl,r}}|m^{\dagger}\widetilde{e_{ij,s}}}_{\psi\otimes\psi}

\displaystyle=\braket{\widetilde{e_{ku,r}}\widetilde{e_{vl,r}}|\widetilde{e_{ij,s}}}_{\psi}=\braket{(Q_{s}^{-1/2})_{uv}\widetilde{e_{kl,r}}|\widetilde{e_{ij,s}}}_{\psi}

\displaystyle=\overline{(Q_{s}^{-1/2})_{uv}}\delta_{ij,s}^{kl,r}=(Q_{s}^{-1/2})_{vu}\delta_{ij,s}^{kl,r}.

∎

Remark 2.7
.

Brannan et al.
[
8
, Lemma 3.2]
uses another unnormalized orthogonal basis

\{f_{ij,s}\coloneqq Q_{s}^{-1/2}e_{ij,s}Q_{s}^{-1/2}\}

for diagonal
Q
in order to simplify the expression of
m^{\dagger}
and prevent the square root
Q^{1/2}
from appearing in the coefficients above.
In this paper, we choose
\{\widetilde{e_{ij,s}}\}
because we later use matrix expressions of operators with respect to this ONB to compute quantum automorphism groups.

Proposition 2.8

(Banica
[
3
, section 1]
)
.

In this terminology,
\psi
is a
\delta
-form on
B

if and only if
\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits_{s}(Q_{s}^{-1})=\delta^{2}
holds for all indices
s
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.6
,
\psi
is a
\delta
-form
if and only if it holds for all
i,j,s
that

\displaystyle\delta^{2}\widetilde{e_{ijs}}

\displaystyle=mm^{\dagger}\widetilde{e_{ijs}}=m\sum_{u,v\leq n_{s}}(Q_{s}^{-1/2})_{vu}\widetilde{e_{iu,s}}\otimes\widetilde{e_{vj,s}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{u,v\leq n_{s}}(Q_{s}^{-1/2})_{vu}(Q_{s}^{-1/2})_{uv}\widetilde{e_{ij,s}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\leq n_{s}}(Q_{s}^{-1})_{vv}\widetilde{e_{ij,s}}=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits_{s}(Q_{s}^{-1})\widetilde{e_{ij,s}},

i.e.,
\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits_{s}(Q_{s}^{-1})=\delta^{2}
for all
s
.
∎

.

Lemma 2.10
.

A
\delta
-form
\psi
on
B
satisfies
\delta^{2}\geq{\left\lvert{B}\right\rvert}=\dim B
,
with equality if and only if
\psi
is tracial.

Proof.

For
\psi=\bigoplus_{s}\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits_{s}(Q_{s}\ \cdot)
as above,
Cauchy-Schwarz inequality with respect to
\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits_{s}
gives us

n_{s}^{2}=(\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits_{s}(Q_{s}^{-1/2}Q_{s}^{1/2}))^{2}\leq\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits_{s}(Q_{s}^{-1})\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits_{s}(Q_{s})\overset{(\textrm{Proposition \ref{prop:deltaform}})}{=}\delta^{2}\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits_{s}(Q_{s}).

Hence
1=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits(Q)\geq\sum_{s}n_{s}^{2}/\delta^{2}={\left\lvert{B}\right\rvert}/\delta^{2}
shows

\delta^{2}\geq{\left\lvert{B}\right\rvert}
, with equality if and only if

Q_{s}^{1/2}=q_{s}Q_{s}^{-1/2}\iff Q_{s}=q_{s}1_{s}

for some constant
q_{s}
for every
s
, i.e.,
\psi
is tracial.
∎

Proposition 2.11

(Banica
[
2
, Proposition 2.1]
)
.

There exists a unique tracial
\delta
-form
\tau
on
B
,
and then
\delta^{2}={\left\lvert{B}\right\rvert}
.

\tau
is explicitly given by
\tau=\bigoplus_{s}\frac{n_{s}}{{\left\lvert{B}\right\rvert}}\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits_{s}

with
Q_{s}=\frac{n_{s}}{{\left\lvert{B}\right\rvert}}1_{s}
.
Moreover
\tau
is the so-called
Plancherel trace
, the restriction of the unique tracial state of
B(L^{2}(B,\psi))
via left regular representation
B\hookrightarrow B(L^{2}(B,\psi))
.

Proof.

Let
\tau=\bigoplus_{s}\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits_{s}(Q_{s}\ \cdot)
be
a tracial
\delta
-form on
B=\bigoplus_{s}M_{n_{s}}
.
Traciality implies
Q_{s}=q_{s}1_{s}
for some
q_{s}>0
for each
s
,
and hence
\delta^{2}=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits_{s}(Q_{s}^{-1})=q_{s}^{-1}n_{s}
by Proposition
2.8
.
Then

1=\tau(1)=\sum_{s}\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits_{s}(q_{s}1_{s})=\sum_{s}q_{s}n_{s}=\sum_{s}n_{s}^{2}/\delta^{2}=\frac{{\left\lvert{B}\right\rvert}}{\delta^{2}},

therefore we have
\delta^{2}={\left\lvert{B}\right\rvert}
and
Q_{s}=\frac{n_{s}}{{\left\lvert{B}\right\rvert}}1_{s}
.

[
2
, Proposition 2.1]
states that
a tracial state
\tau
satisfies
mm^{\dagger}=\delta^{2}\mathrm{id}

if and only if
\tau
is the ristriction of the unique tracial state of
B(L^{2}(B,\psi))
.
∎

Remark 2.12
.

Since commutativity
xy=yx
implies traciality
\tau(xy)=\tau(yx)
, a commutative quantum set is the pair
(\mathbb{C}^{n},\tau)

of an
n\times n
diagonal matrix algebra
\mathbb{C}^{n}
and its normalized trace
\tau=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits/n
, which corresponds to the pair of an
n
-element set and the uniform probability measure as in Lemma
2.4
.

In string diagram notation, involution and adjoint are related via twisted wires.
The equality
x^{\dagger}=\bra{x}=\psi(x^{*}\cdot)=\psi m(x^{*}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B})
shows the identity

Therefore
f(x^{*})^{*}=f(x)\ \forall x\in B
is exactly equal to the desired equality.
∎

Remark 2.14
.

Note that bending strings in the other direction can result in different operators, like

Proposition 2.15
.

Given
*
-preserving operator
f:B\to B
,
f^{\dagger}
is also
*
-preserving if and only if
f
commutes with

\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox to8.94pt{\vbox to14.63pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 8.73582pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{}{{}}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}}{{{}}{{}}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 0 6.01 -11.81 15.85 -11.81 9.84 C -11.81 3.83 0 13.68 0 19.69}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{array}=Q^{-1}(\cdot)Q
.

Proof.

If

is also

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.98613pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{}{}{}{}}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{array}\overset{\textrm{(\ref{eqn:f'*-pres})}}{=}f^{\dagger}\overset{\textrm{(\ref{eqn:f*-pres})}}{=}\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox to38.34pt{\vbox to42.25pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 19.1685pt\lower-21.1261pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 10.14 0 C 10.14 5.6 5.6 10.14 0 10.14 C -5.6 10.14 -10.14 5.6 -10.14 0 C -10.14 -5.6 -5.6 -10.14 0 -10.14 C 5.6 -10.14 10.14 -5.6 10.14 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

Proof.

The equality about
\mathrm{id}_{H}
is by the identification.
Since the multiplication in
B(H)
is the composition, the equality about
m
directly follows from the snake equation (
2.1
).
Let
\{v_{i}\}_{i}
be an ONB for
H
. Then

\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox to45.36pt{\vbox to25.29pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.34062pt\lower-9.56645pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{{}{}{}{}}}{{}{}{}{}}}{}

Note that
H\otimes H^{*}
is equipped with the inner product

\displaystyle\braket{v_{1}\otimes w_{1}^{\dagger}|v_{0}\otimes w_{0}^{\dagger}}_{H\otimes H^{*}}

\displaystyle=\braket{v_{1}|v_{0}}_{H}\braket{w_{1}^{\dagger}|w_{0}^{\dagger}}_{H^{*}}=\braket{v_{1}|v_{0}}_{H}\braket{w_{0}|w_{1}}_{H}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits\left(\ket{w_{1}}\bra{v_{1}}\ket{v_{0}}\bra{w_{0}}\right)=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits\left((\ket{v_{1}}\bra{w_{1}})^{\dagger}(\ket{v_{0}}\bra{w_{0}})\right)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\ (\ket{v_{1}}\bra{w_{1}})\ \middle|\ (\ket{v_{0}}\bra{w_{0}})\ \right\rangle_{\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits},

Proof.

The unit and the multiplication are those in Corollary
2.17
.
In the same way as Proposition
2.16
,
we have
\langle\cdot|\cdot\rangle_{\delta^{2}\widetilde{\psi}}=\langle\cdot|\cdot\rangle_{\psi\otimes\psi}
, and hence
\widetilde{\psi}
is faithful and
m_{B(B)}^{\dagger}
with respect to
\widetilde{\psi}
is as stated.
Since
\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox to14.63pt{\vbox to14.63pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 14.42638pt\lower-7.31319pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{{}{}{}{}}}
{{{}{}{}{}}}
{{{}{}{}{}}}
{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 0 5.44 -4.41 9.84 -9.84 9.84 C -15.28 9.84 -19.69 5.44 -19.69 0 C -19.69 -5.44 -15.28 -9.84 -9.84 -9.84 C -4.41 -9.84 0 -5.44 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{array}=\delta^{2}
,
\widetilde{\psi}
is a state and
we have
m_{B(B)}m_{B(B)}^{\dagger}=(\delta^{2})^{2}\mathrm{id}_{B(B)}
.
∎

Note that
m_{B}^{\dagger}:B\to B\otimes B
with
m_{B(B)}
as above is a
*
-homomorphism that corresponds to the left regular representation of
B
(cf. Vicary
[
17
, Lemma 3.19, 3.20]
).
If we identify
L^{2}(B,\psi)^{*}
with the left tensorand
B
instead of the right one, then
m_{B}^{\dagger}
corresponds to the right regular representation.
The Frobenius equality (
2.1
) means that the left and right regular representations are
*
-homomorphisms.

2.2
Quantum graphs

Recall that a simple (i.e., without multiple edges) finite classical graph is a pair
(V,E)
of finite vertex set
V
and edge set
E\subseteq V\times V
, and the adjacency operator of
(V,E)
is the matrix
A\in M_{V}(\mathbb{C})\cong B(C(V))
such that the
(v,w)
entry
A_{v,w}=1
if
(v,w)\in E
and
A_{v,w}=0
otherwise,
where
M_{V}(\mathbb{C})
denotes the matrix algebra whose raws and columns are indexed by
V
.
This notion of adjacency operator is generalized on quantum sets as follows.

Definition 2.19

(Musto, Reutter, Verdon
[
13
, Definition 5.1]
, Brannan et al.
[
7
, Definition 3.4]
)
.

We define a
quantum adjacency operator
on a quantum set
(B,\psi)
as an operator
A:B\to B
satisfying
Schur idempotence

Remark 2.20
.

In the classical case
(\mathbb{C}^{n},\tau)
,

Schur product

f\bullet g
of operators
f,g\in M_{n}\cong B(\mathbb{C}^{n})
is defined as the entrywise product. In fact it is realized as

f\bullet g=m(f\otimes g)m^{\dagger}/\delta^{2}.

That is why the condition (
2.19
) is called Schur idempotence. Since a matrix is Schur idempotent if and only if it is
\{0,1\}
-valued, quantum graphs
A
on
(\mathbb{C}^{n},\tau)
are exactly equal to the adjacency operators of classical multiplicity-free graphs on
n
vertices.
Then
A
is always real because
A
is real-valued and
*
is just a complex conjugate, and
A
is undirected if and only if the graph
(V,E)
is undirected, i.e., any edge
(v,w)\in E
has its opposite
(w,v)\in E
.
It seems natural to call the graph symmetric instead of self-transpose, but it is confusing with symmetricity (traciality) of a quantum set, so we use the term self-transpose.
A classical graph is called reflexive if it has all self-loops
(v,v)
for
v\in V
, and irreflexive if it has no self-loop.
Thus the reflexivity is characterized by the Schur product of
A
and
\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}
,
which outputs the diagonal entries
A_{v,v}
.

Remark 2.21
.

The notion of quantum adjacency operator is first introduced by
Musto, Reutter, Verdon
[
13
, Definition 5.1]
,
who defined undirected quantum graphs on tracial quantum sets.
Following
[
13
]
, Brannan et al.
[
7
, Definition 3.4]
defined
undirected quantum graphs on general quantum sets.
The weakest definition assigning only Schur idempotence appears in Brannan, Eifler, Voigt, Weber
[
8
, Definition 3.3]
.

Lemma 2.22
.

Let
(B,\psi,A)
be a quantum graph.
Every couple of the following three conditions imply the other.
Equivalently, all couples of the following are equivalent to each other.

(1)

A
is self-adjoint;

(2)

A
is self-transpose;

(3)

A
is real.

In particular,
A
is undirected if and only if (1), (2), and (3) hold.

Proof.

∎

Recall that an operator
A:B\to B^{\prime}
between
C^{*}
-algebras is called positive if it preserves the positive cone consisting of positive semidefinite elements
A(B^{+})\subset{B^{\prime}}^{+}
, and called completely positive if its amplification
A\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M_{n}}:B\otimes M_{n}\cong M_{n}(B)\to B^{\prime}\otimes M_{n}\cong M_{n}(B^{\prime})
is positive for arbitrary positive integer
n
.
We can deduce the following equivalence from Schur idempotence.

Proposition 2.23
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(B,\psi,A)
be a quantum graph. TFAE:

(1)

A
is real;

(2)

A
is positive;

(3)

A
is completely positive.

Proof.

(3)\implies(2)
Obvious by definition.
(2)\implies(1)
Since the positive cone
B^{+}
of
B
spans the subspace
B^{sa}
of self-adjoint operators,
A(B^{+})\subset B^{+}
implies
A(B^{sa})\subset B^{sa}
, i.e.,

A
is real.

(1)\implies(3)

Assume that
A
is a real quantum graph on
(B,\psi)
.
Then
A\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M_{n}}
is also a quantum graph on
(B\otimes M_{n},\psi\otimes\tau_{M_{n}})
for arbitrary
n
, and it is real since the involution is tensorandwise
(b\otimes x)^{*}=b^{*}\otimes x^{*}
in
B\otimes M_{n}
.
Replacing
(B\otimes M_{n},\psi\otimes\tau_{M_{n}},A\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M_{n}})
by
(B,\psi,A)
, it suffices to show that
A
is positive. We take an arbitrary
x\in B
and check that
A(x^{*}x)
is positive semidefinite:

\displaystyle=\delta^{-2}\sum_{i}A(x^{*}b_{i})A(b_{i}^{*}x)=\delta^{-2}\sum_{i}A(b_{i}^{*}x)^{*}A(b_{i}^{*}x)\geq 0.

Therefore
A
is positive.
∎

Recall that the
indegree
(resp,
outdegree
) of a vertex of a classical directed graph is the number of edges into (resp. out of) the vertex, and the graph is called
d
-regular
if the indegrees and outdegrees of all vertices are equal to
d
. Note that indegree and outdegree coincide and are called the degree if the graph is undirected. Recall also that a classical graph is
d
-regular if and only if the adjacency operator
A
and
A^{\dagger}
have the constant function
1
as an eigenvector of eigenvalue
d
. This notion can be generalized for quantum graphs:

Definition 2.24
.

A quantum graph
\mathcal{G}=(B,\psi,A)
is
d
-regular
if

A1=d1\text{ and }\psi A=d\psi

are satisfied, and then
d
is called the
degree
of
\mathcal{G}
.

Here we exhibit typical examples of quantum graphs.

Example 2.25

(cf. Brannan et al.
[
7
, Remark 3.6]
)
.

•

Let
(V,E)
be a simple classical graph, and
A:C(V)\to C(V)
the adjacency matrix. Then
(C(V),\tau,A)
is a quantum graph.

•

Let
(B,\psi)
be a quantum set. The (reflexive)
complete graph
on
(B,\psi)
is given by
A=\delta^{2}\psi(\cdot)1
, which is an undirected reflexive
\delta^{2}
-regular quantum graph. Indeed the definition of the unit and counit shows

In classical caces
{\left\lvert{B}\right\rvert}\tau(\cdot)1=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits(\cdot)1
is the matrix with all entries one, which is the reflexive complete graph.
By Proposition
2.26
, the irreflexive quantum complete graph is

A=\delta^{2}\psi(\cdot)1-\mathrm{id}_{B}
.

•

Let
(B,\psi)
be a quantum set. The
trivial graph
on
(B,\psi)
is given by
A=\mathrm{id}_{B}
, which is an undirected reflexive
1
-regular quantum graph.
This follows from the specialty (
2.2
) and snake equation (
2.1
).
The trivial graph is the reflexive complement (defined in Proposition
2.28
) of the complete graph.
In classical cases, the trivial graph is the graph with only the self-loops.
Its irreflexive counterpart as in Proposition
2.26
is
A=0
.

Proposition 2.26
.

Irreflexive real quantum graphs
A_{\mathrm{irref}}
on
(B,\psi)
have one to one correspondence with reflexive real quantum graphs
A_{\mathrm{ref}}
via

A_{\mathrm{irref}}+\mathrm{id}_{B}=A_{\mathrm{ref}}.

Thus their spectra satisfy

\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits(A_{\mathrm{irref}})+1=\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits(A_{\mathrm{ref}})
.

Remark 2.27
.

The correspondence between reflexive and irreflexive quantum graphs also holds for self-transpose quantum graphs. Its proof is the same except the replacement of real condition by self-transpose condition.

Proof.

Let
A=A_{\mathrm{irref}}
be an irreflexive real quantum graph on
(B,\psi)
.
We show that
A_{\mathrm{ref}}=A+\mathrm{id}_{B}
is a reflexive real quantum graph.
The reality of
A
and
\mathrm{id}_{B}
shows that
A_{\mathrm{ref}}
is also real by linearity.
The reflexivity follows by

where the third equality follows from topological calculation using the coassociativity, associativity, snake equation (
2.1
) and Frobenius equation (
2.1
).
Therefore
A_{\mathrm{ref}}=A_{\mathrm{irref}}+\mathrm{id}_{B}
is a reflexive quantum graph.
Similarly given reflexive quantum graph
A_{\mathrm{ref}}
, it follows that
A_{\mathrm{irref}}=A_{\mathrm{ref}}-\mathrm{id}_{B}
is an irreflexive quantum graph.
The equality of their spectra follows from

\lambda\mathrm{id}_{B}-A_{\mathrm{irref}}=(\lambda+1)\mathrm{id}_{B}-A_{\mathrm{ref}}\quad\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{C}.

∎

Proposition 2.28
.

Let
(B,\psi,A)
be a real reflexive quantum graph. Then

A^{c}\coloneqq\mathrm{id}_{B}+\delta^{2}\psi(\cdot)1-A

is also a real reflexive quantum graph on
(B,\psi)
, the so-called reflexive complement of
A
.

Proof.

Since
\mathrm{id}_{B}
,
\delta^{2}\psi(\cdot)1
, and
A
are real reflexive quantum graphs on
(B,\psi)
, linearity shows that
A^{c}
is also real and reflexive.
We have by distributing the unit and the counit that

2.3
Quantum isomorphisms

Definition 2.29

(Musto, Reutter, Verdon
[
13
, Definition 3.11, 4.3]
)
.

A
quantum function

(H,P):(B^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\to(B,\psi)
between quantum sets
(B,\psi)
and
(B^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})
is a pair

(H,P)
of a finite dimensional Hilbert space
H
and a linear operator

which respectively means that
P
preserves the counit and comultiplication.
If
{\left\lvert{H}\right\rvert}=\dim H=1
, then a quantum function (resp. quantum bijection)
(H,P)
is called a classical function (resp. classical bijection).

Remark 2.30
.

In the case of
{\left\lvert{H}\right\rvert}=1
, we may forget the oriented strings of
H
.
Then (
2.29
) exactly says that

\displaystyle P(1)

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle P(x)P(y)

\displaystyle=P(xy),

\displaystyle P(x^{*})^{*}

\displaystyle=P(x)\quad\forall x,y\in B,

i.e.,
P:B\to B^{\prime}
is a
*
-homomorphism. Similarly (
2.29
) says that
P:B\to B^{\prime}
is a cohomomorphism. This is why
(H,P)
is called classical if
{\left\lvert{H}\right\rvert}=1
.

Note that the quantum function
(H,P):(B^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\to(B,\psi)
and ‘homomorphism’
P:B\otimes H\to H\otimes B^{\prime}
have opposite direction. This is based on the Gelfand duality, where a set function
f:X\to Y
corresponds to a unital
*
-homomorphism
\cdot\circ f:C(Y)\to C(X)
.

Remark 2.31
.

Alternatively we may consider

\widetilde{P}=\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox to14.63pt{\vbox to43.48pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.31319pt\lower-21.73956pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

) formulates that

is a

do not work well for infinite dimensional
H
,
the formulation in terms of
\widetilde{P}
is valid.
The formulation of quantum isomorphisms by Brannan et al.

[
7
, section 4]
is derived from this viewpoint.

Remark 2.32
.

By the snake equations (
2.1
),
the
*
-preserving condition in (

Definition 2.33

(Musto, Reutter, Verdon

, Definition 3.18]

The category
\mathop{\mathrm{QSet}}\nolimits
of quantum sets is defined as a
2
-category that
consists of

•

Objects:
quantum sets
(B,\psi)
;

•

1-morphisms:
quantum functions
(H,P):(B^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\to(B,\psi)
;

•

2-morphisms:
intertwiners
f:(H,P)\to(H^{\prime},P^{\prime})
.

Given quantum sets
(B,\psi),(B^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})
, we define the category
\mathop{\mathrm{QBij}}\nolimits((B^{\prime},\psi^{\prime}),(B,\psi))
as a category consisting of

•

Objects:
quantum bijections
(H,P):(B^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\to(B,\psi)
;

•

Morphisms:
intertwiners
f:(H,P)\to(H^{\prime},P^{\prime})
.

Lemma 2.34

(Tracial case by Musto, Reutter, Verdon
[
13
, Theorem 4.8]
)
.

For a quantum function
(H,P):(B^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\to(B,\psi)
, TFAE:

(1)

(H,P)
is a quantum bijection;

(2)

P
is a unitary operator.

Proof.

(1)\implies(2)

By the involution and multiplication preserving conditions in (
2.29
)
and the counit preserving condition in (
2.29
), we have

\displaystyle P^{\dagger}P=\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox to28.85pt{\vbox to57.31pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{{}}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{{}}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

and taking the adjoint again, we obtain (
2.29
):

Therefore
(H,P)
is a quantum bijection.
∎

Definition 2.35

(Musto, Reutter, Verdon
[
13
, Definition 5.11]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(B,\psi,A)
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=(B^{\prime},\psi^{\prime},A^{\prime})
be quantum graphs.
A
quantum
(resp.
classical
)
isomorphism

(H,P):\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{G}
is a quantum (resp. classical) bijection
(H,P):(B^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\to(B,\psi)
satisfying

Quantum graphs
\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
are said to be quantum (resp. classical) isomorphic if there is a nonzero quantum (resp. classical) isomorphism
(H,P):\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{G}
.

Remark 2.36
.

Quantum isomorphism is denoted by
\cong_{q}
.
Recall that we assume
H
to be finite-dimensional.
If quantum graphs are quantum isomorphic via possibly infinite dimensional
H
, then they are said to be
C^{*}
-algebraically quantum isomorphic
(
\cong_{C^{*}}
) in Brannan et al.
[
7
, Definition 4.4]
. The authors of
[
7
]
also defined
quantum commuting isomorphism
(
\cong_{qc}
), and
algebraic quantum isomorphism
(
\cong_{A^{*}}
). For quantum graphs,
\cong_{q},\cong_{qc}\Rightarrow\cong_{C^{*}}\Leftrightarrow\cong_{A^{*}}
(
[
7
, Corollary 4.8]
).

Since quantum bijections are unitary, finiteness of
{\left\lvert{H}\right\rvert}
implies
{\left\lvert{B}\right\rvert}={\left\lvert{B^{\prime}}\right\rvert}
for
(B,\psi,A)\cong_{q}(B^{\prime},\psi^{\prime},A^{\prime})
.
It is shown in
[
7
, Example 4.13]
that there are
C^{*}
-quantum isomorphic quantum graphs with distinct dimensions, hence our
\cong_{q}
is strictly stronger than
\cong_{C^{*}}
.

Definition 2.37
.

Given quantum graphs
\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, the category

\mathop{\mathrm{QIso}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\mathcal{G})
of quantum isomorphisms is a category that consists of

•

Objects:
quantum isomorphisms
(H,P):\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{G}
;

•

Morphisms:
intertwiners
f:(H,P)\to(H^{\prime},P^{\prime})
.

We denote
\mathop{\mathrm{QIso}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G})
by
\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G})
.

Remark 2.38
.

Since tensoring with zero annihilates everything, any couple of quantum graphs have a trivial quantum isomorphism
0=(P=0,H=0)
.

Remark 2.39
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(B,\psi,A),\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=(B^{\prime},\psi^{\prime},A^{\prime})
be quantum graphs,
and
\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m},\{e^{\prime}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{n}
be ONB’s for
L^{2}(B,\psi),L^{2}(B^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})
with
{\left\lvert{B}\right\rvert}=m,{\left\lvert{B^{\prime}}\right\rvert}=n
.

(H,P):\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{G}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{array}.

Then
\widetilde{P}:B\to B^{\prime}\otimes B(H)
as in Remark
2.31
is explicitly described as
\widetilde{P}e_{i}=\sum_{k}e^{\prime}_{k}\otimes P_{i}^{k}
.
In this setting
\widetilde{P}
is a unital
*
-homomorphism since
P
is a quantum function,
and the matrix
(P_{i}^{k})_{k,i}\in M_{n,m}(B(H))
is unitary since
P
is a quantum bijection (hence unitary by Lemma
2.34
),
and
\widetilde{P}A=(A^{\prime}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B(H)})\widetilde{P}
since
P
is a quantum isomorphism.
Note that
m,n
need not be equal if we allow infinite-dimensional
H
.
By considering universal such
P_{i}^{k}
’s, we reach the notion of the quantum automorphism group of a quantum graph as below and the bigalois extention between two quantum graphs introduced in
[
7
, Definition 4.1]
, which we later use in section 4.

Definition 2.40

(Woronowicz
[
20
, Definition 1.1]
,
[
21
, Definition 1.1]
)
.

A
compact quantum group
(CQG) is a pair
(\mathcal{A},\Delta)
of a separable unital
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
and a
*
-homomorphism
\Delta:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}
, so-called comultiplication, satisfying

(coassociativity)

(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{A}})\Delta=(\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\Delta)\Delta
;

(cancellation property)

(\mathcal{A}\otimes 1)\Delta(\mathcal{A})

and

(1\otimes\mathcal{A})\Delta(\mathcal{A})

are dense in
\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}
.

Definition 2.41

(Brannan et al.
[
7
, Definition 3.7]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(B,\psi,A)
be a quantum graph and fix an ONB
\{e_{i}\}_{i}
for
L^{2}(B,\psi)
.
The quantum automorphism group of
\mathcal{G}
is
a CQG
\mathop{\mathrm{Qut}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G})=({C(\mathrm{Qut}(\mathcal{G}))},\Delta)
defined as follows:

•

The group algebra
{C(\mathrm{Qut}(\mathcal{G}))}
is the universal unital
C^{*}
-algebra generated by the coefficients
u_{i}^{k}
of a unitary
u=(u_{i}^{k})_{k,i}\in M_{n}({C(\mathrm{Qut}(\mathcal{G}))})
that makes the operator

\rho:B\ni e_{i}\mapsto\sum_{k}e_{k}\otimes u_{i}^{k}\in B\otimes{C(\mathrm{Qut}(\mathcal{G}))}

a unital
*
-homomorphism satisfying
\rho\ A=(A\otimes\mathrm{id})\rho
. This
\rho
and
u
are called the fundamental representation;

•

The comultiplication
\Delta:{C(\mathrm{Qut}(\mathcal{G}))}\to{C(\mathrm{Qut}(\mathcal{G}))}\otimes{C(\mathrm{Qut}(\mathcal{G}))}
is defined as a
*
-homomorphism satisfying

\Delta u_{i}^{k}=\sum_{j}u_{j}^{k}\otimes u_{i}^{j}.

We have additional operators associated to
{C(\mathrm{Qut}(\mathcal{G}))}
, a counit
\epsilon
and antipode
S
defined as a
*
-homomorphism
\epsilon:{C(\mathrm{Qut}(\mathcal{G}))}\to\mathbb{C}
and a homomorphism
S:{C(\mathrm{Qut}(\mathcal{G}))}\to{C(\mathrm{Qut}(\mathcal{G}))}^{op}

satisfying

\epsilon u_{i}^{k}=\delta_{ik};\qquad Su_{i}^{k}={u_{k}^{i}}^{*}.

Then
({C(\mathrm{Qut}(\mathcal{G}))},\Delta,\epsilon,S)
satisfy

\displaystyle(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id})\Delta

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta)\Delta;

\displaystyle(\epsilon\otimes\mathrm{id})\Delta

\displaystyle=\mathrm{id}=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\epsilon)\Delta;

\displaystyle m(\epsilon\otimes\mathrm{id})\Delta

\displaystyle=\epsilon(\cdot)1=m(\mathrm{id}\otimes\epsilon)\Delta.

Such a quadruple
({C(\mathrm{Qut}(\mathcal{G}))},\Delta,\epsilon,S)
is called a Hopf
*
-algebra.

The authors of
[
13
]
investigated the relationship between the category
\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G})
and the quantum automorphism group
\mathop{\mathrm{Qut}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G})
for classical graphs, but they did not introduce the quantum automorphism group of quantum graphs. Here we show the straightforward generalization of the following theorem.

Theorem 2.42

(Musto, Reutter, Verdon,
[
13
, Proposition 5.19]
)
.

If
\mathcal{G}
is a classical graph on
(\mathbb{C}^{n},\tau)
, then we have an isomorphism of categories

\mathop{\mathrm{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathrm{fin}}({C(\mathrm{Qut}(\mathcal{G}))})\cong\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G})

where
\mathop{\mathrm{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathrm{fin}}({C(\mathrm{Qut}(\mathcal{G}))})
is the category of finite dimensional
*
-representations of the
C^{*}
-algebra
{C(\mathrm{Qut}(\mathcal{G}))}
, and
\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G})
is the category of quantum automorphisms on
\mathcal{G}
.

Theorem 2.43
.

If
G
is a quantum graph on
(B,\psi)
, then we have an isomorphism of categories

\mathop{\mathrm{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathrm{fin}}({C(\mathrm{Qut}(\mathcal{G}))})\cong\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G})

where
\mathop{\mathrm{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathrm{fin}}({C(\mathrm{Qut}(\mathcal{G}))})
is the category of finite dimensional
*
-representations of the
C^{*}
-algebra
{C(\mathrm{Qut}(\mathcal{G}))}
, and
\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G})
is the category of quantum automorphisms on
\mathcal{G}
.

Proof.

As is explained in Remark
2.39
, the CQG algebra
{C(\mathrm{Qut}(\mathcal{G}))}
is generated by the universal coefficients of a unitary
u=(u_{i}^{k})
that satisfies exactly the same relation as the unitary
P=(P_{i}^{k})
of a quantum automorphism
(H,P)
on
\mathcal{G}=(B,\psi,A)
.
Therefore
given a quantum isomorphism
(H,P)
in
\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G})
, the universality of
{C(\mathrm{Qut}(\mathcal{G}))}
shows the existence of a
*
-representation
\pi_{P}:{C(\mathrm{Qut}(\mathcal{G}))}\ni u_{i}^{k}\mapsto P_{i}^{k}\in B(H)
.
Conversely a
*
-representation
\pi:{C(\mathrm{Qut}(\mathcal{G}))}\to B(H)
defines operators

P_{i}^{k}=\pi(u_{i}^{k})
, which induces a quantum automorphism
P_{\pi}=\sum_{j}\ket{e_{k}}P_{i}^{k}\bra{e_{i}}
.
By construction, it is trivial that
P_{\pi_{P}}=P
and
\pi_{P_{\pi}}=\pi
.
For quantum automorphisms
(H,P),(H^{\prime},P^{\prime})
,
an operator
f:H\to H^{\prime}
is an intertwiner
(H,P)\to(H^{\prime},P^{\prime})
in
\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G})

\iff

(f\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B})P=P^{\prime}(\mathrm{id}_{B}\otimes f)

\iff

f\pi(u_{i}^{k})=fP_{i}^{k}={P^{\prime}}_{i}^{k}f=\pi^{\prime}(u_{i}^{k})f\ (\forall i,k)

\iff

f\pi(\cdot)=\pi^{\prime}(\cdot)f

\iff

f
is an intertwiner
\pi\to\pi^{\prime}
in
\mathop{\mathrm{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathrm{fin}}({C(\mathrm{Qut}(\mathcal{G}))})
.
Therefore the intertwiners also coincide.
∎

Since finiteness of
{\left\lvert{H}\right\rvert}
is not used in the proof of Theorem
2.43
, if we allow ‘
\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G})
’ to include infinite dimensional quantum isomorphisms as in Remark
2.39
,
then
\mathop{\mathrm{Rep}}\nolimits({C(\mathrm{Qut}(\mathcal{G}))})\cong\text{`}\mathop{\mathrm{QAut}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G})\text{'}
is obtained.

3
Quantum graphs on
M_{2}

3.1
Tracial quantum graphs

Let
(B=\bigoplus_{s}M_{n_{s}},\tau)
be a quantum set with the unique tracial
\sqrt{{\left\lvert{B}\right\rvert}}
-form
\tau=\frac{1}{{\left\lvert{B}\right\rvert}}\bigoplus_{s}n_{s}\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits_{s}
.
We always assume that
quantum graphs
are undirected in this chapter.

Let
A=(A_{ij,s}^{kl,r})_{i,j\leq n_{s},s}^{k,l\leq n_{r},r}
be a reflexive quantum graph on
(B,\tau)
parametrized as

A_{ij,s}^{kl,r}=\braket{\widetilde{e_{kl,r}}|A\widetilde{e_{ij,s}}}\textrm{ , i.e., }A=\sum_{ijsklr}\ket{\widetilde{e_{kl,r}}}A_{ij,s}^{kl,r}\bra{\widetilde{e_{ij,s}}}

where
\left\{\widetilde{e_{ij,s}}=\sqrt{\frac{{\left\lvert{B}\right\rvert}}{n_{s}}}e_{ij,s}\right\}
is an ONB for
L^{2}(B,\tau)
.
Thus
A
is a self-adjoint (
\overline{A_{ij,s}^{kl,r}}=A^{ij,s}_{kl,r}
) operator satisfying the following:

Schur idempotent

\displaystyle\iff\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{s}n_{r}}}\sum_{u,v}A_{iu,s}^{kv,r}A_{uj,s}^{vl,r}=A_{ij,s}^{kl,r};

(3.1)

reflexive

\displaystyle\iff\frac{1}{n_{s}}\sum_{u}A_{iu,s}^{ku,s}=\delta_{ik};

(3.2)

undirected (self-transpose)

\displaystyle\iff A_{ij,s}^{kl,r}=A_{lk,r}^{ji,s},

(3.3)

where the RHS of these equivalences are quantified by
\forall i,j,s,k,l,r
.

Note that these relations are independent for different pairs
(r,s)
and
(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})
.

3.2
Tracial quantum graphs on
M_{2}

Let
A=(A_{ij}^{kl})_{i,j=1,2}^{k,l=1,2}
be a quantum adjacency matrix on
(M_{2},\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits/2)
with respect to the orthonormal basis

\left\{\widetilde{e_{ij}}=\sqrt{2}e_{ij}\right\}
.
Then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(A_{i1}^{k1}A_{1j}^{1l}+A_{i1}^{k2}A_{1j}^{2l}+A_{i2}^{k1}A_{2j}^{1l}+A_{i2}^{k2}A_{2j}^{2l}\right)

\displaystyle=A_{ij}^{kl}\quad\forall i,j,k,l=1,2;

(3.4)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(A_{i1}^{k1}+A_{i2}^{k2}\right)

\displaystyle=\delta_{ik}\quad\forall i,k=1,2;

(3.5)

\displaystyle\overline{A^{ij}_{kl}}=A_{ij}^{kl}

\displaystyle=A_{lk}^{ji}\quad\forall i,j,k,l=1,2.

(3.6)

By the latter two conditions (
3.5
)(
3.6
),
A
is of the following form where
x,p\in\mathbb{R}
and
y,z\in\mathbb{C}
:

\begin{pmatrix}A^{11}_{11}&A^{11}_{12}&A^{11}_{21}&A^{11}_{22}\\
A^{12}_{11}&A^{12}_{12}&A^{12}_{21}&A^{12}_{22}\\
A^{21}_{11}&A^{21}_{12}&A^{21}_{21}&A^{21}_{22}\\
A^{22}_{11}&A^{22}_{12}&A^{22}_{21}&A^{22}_{22}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}p&\overline{y}&y&x\\
y&2-p&z&-y\\
\overline{y}&\overline{z}&2-p&-\overline{y}\\
x&-\overline{y}&-y&p\end{pmatrix}

Regularity
A1=d1
holds for some
d\in\mathbb{R}
if and only if

\displaystyle d1=A(e_{11}+e_{22})

\displaystyle=(A_{11}^{11}+A_{22}^{11})e_{11}+(A_{11}^{12}+A_{22}^{12})e_{12}+(A_{11}^{21}+A_{22}^{21})e_{21}+(A_{11}^{22}+A_{22}^{22})e_{22}

\displaystyle=(p+x)e_{11}+(\overline{y}-\overline{y})e_{12}+(y-y)e_{21}+(x+p)e_{22}=(p+x)1,

i.e., this is automatically
p+x=d
-regular.

If
y=0
, then we have
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits(A)=\{p\pm x,2-p\pm{\left\lvert{z}\right\rvert}\}
.

Theorem 3.1
.

A reflexive quantum graph
A
on
(M_{2},\tau)
is classical (and quantum) isomorphic to exactly one of the following
d
-regular quantum graphs.

d=1)

Trivial graph
A_{1}=\mathrm{id}_{B}={\begin{pmatrix}1&&&\\
&1&&\\
&&1&\\
&&&1\end{pmatrix}}
,
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits(A_{1})=\{1,1,1,1\}
.

d=2)

A_{2}={\begin{pmatrix}2&&&\\
&0&&\\
&&0&\\
&&&2\end{pmatrix}}
,
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits(A_{2})=\{2,2,0,0\}
.

d=3)

A_{3}={\begin{pmatrix}1&&&2\\
&1&&\\
&&1&\\
2&&&1\end{pmatrix}}
,
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits(A_{3})=\{3,1,1,-1\}
.

d=4)

Complete graph
A_{4}=4\tau(\cdot)1={\begin{pmatrix}2&&&2\\
&0&&\\
&&0&\\
2&&&2\end{pmatrix}}
,
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits(A_{4})=\{4,0,0,0\}
.

Proof.

By Schur idempotence (
3.4
), we get the following equations:

\displaystyle 2p

\displaystyle=p^{2}+{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}^{2}+(2-p)^{2}+{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}^{2}

(3.7)

\displaystyle 2(2-p)

\displaystyle=p(2-p)-{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}^{2}+p(2-p)-{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}^{2}\iff(p-1)(2-p)={\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}^{2}

(3.8)

\displaystyle 2x

\displaystyle={\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}^{2}+x^{2}+{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}^{2}+{\left\lvert{z}\right\rvert}^{2}

(3.9)

\displaystyle 2z

\displaystyle=y^{2}+xz+y^{2}+zx\iff(1-x)z=y^{2}

(3.10)

\displaystyle 2y

\displaystyle=py+yx-y(2-p)+\overline{y}z

(3.11)

By (
3.8
) and (
3.10
), we get
p\in[1,2]
and
(p-1)(2-p)={\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}^{2}={\left\lvert{1-x}\right\rvert}{\left\lvert{z}\right\rvert}
. Hence

\displaystyle(\ref{M2-1})

\displaystyle\iff(p-1+2-p)^{2}=1^{2}=1\quad\textrm{(automatic)}

\displaystyle(\ref{M2-3})

\displaystyle\iff({\left\lvert{1-x}\right\rvert}+{\left\lvert{z}\right\rvert})^{2}=1\iff{\left\lvert{1-x}\right\rvert}+{\left\lvert{z}\right\rvert}=1

[0]

If
y=0
, (
3.11
) is automatic,
(\ref{M2-2})\iff p=1
or
2
,
and
(\ref{M2-4})\iff(1-x)z=0
.

•

If
x=1
, then
{\left\lvert{z}\right\rvert}=1
by (
3.9
).

•

If
z=0
, then
{\left\lvert{1-x}\right\rvert}=1
by (
3.9
), hence
x=0,2

Therefore we have the following table under
y=0
:

\begin{array}[]{|c|c|c|c|c|}p&x&z&d=p+x&\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits(A)=\{p\pm x,2-p\pm{\left\lvert{z}\right\rvert}\}\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr 1&0&0&1&\{1,1,1,1\}\\
\hline\cr 1&1&\mathbb{T}&2&\{2,0,2,0\}\\
\hline\cr 1&2&0&3&\{3,-1,1,1\}\\
\hline\cr 2&0&0&2&\{2,2,0,0\}\\
\hline\cr 2&1&\mathbb{T}&3&\{3,1,1,-1\}\\
\hline\cr 2&2&0&4&\{4,0,0,0\}\end{array}

where
\mathbb{T}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid{\left\lvert{z}\right\rvert}=1\}
.

[1]

If
y\neq 0
, (
3.10
) implies
z\neq 0
and hence

\displaystyle(\ref{M2-5})

\displaystyle\iff(x-2(2-p))y+\overline{y}z=0\overset{y\neq 0}{\iff}(x-2(2-p))y^{2}+{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}^{2}z=0

\displaystyle\iff((x-2(2-p))(1-x)+(p-1)(2-p))z=0

\displaystyle\iff(x-(3-p))(x-(2-p))=0\iff x=3-p\textrm{ or }2-p

(3.12)

By (
3.8
), we may put
y=\theta\sqrt{(p-1)(2-p)}

for some
\theta\in\mathbb{T}
.
Then (
3.10
) implies

z=\frac{y^{2}}{1-x}=\theta^{2}\frac{(p-1)(2-p)}{1-x}.

If
x=2-p
in (
3.12
), then
d=p+x=2
and

z=\theta^{2}\frac{(p-1)(2-p)}{p-1}=\theta^{2}(2-p)=\theta^{2}(4-d-p),

which satisfies (
3.9
):
{\left\lvert{1-x}\right\rvert}+{\left\lvert{z}\right\rvert}=(p-1)+(2-p)=1
, hence all conditions are satisfied.

If
x=3-p
in (
3.12
), then
d=p+x=3
and

z=\theta^{2}\frac{(p-1)(2-p)}{p-2}=\theta^{2}(1-p)=\theta^{2}(4-d-p),

which satisfies (
3.9
):
{\left\lvert{1-x}\right\rvert}+{\left\lvert{z}\right\rvert}=(2-p)+(p-1)=1
, hence all conditions are satisfied.

Therefore we obtain two families of quantum graphs for each
d=2,3
parametrized by

(p,\theta)\in(1,2)\times\mathbb{T}
under
{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}=\sqrt{(p-1)(2-p)}\neq 0
:

\displaystyle d=2)

\displaystyle A^{(2)}_{p,\theta}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}p&\overline{\theta}{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}&\theta{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}&2-p\\
\theta{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}&2-p&\theta^{2}(2-p)&-\theta{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}\\
\overline{\theta}{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}&\overline{\theta}^{2}(2-p)&2-p&-\overline{\theta}{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}\\
2-p&-\overline{\theta}{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}&-\theta{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}&p\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle d=3)

\displaystyle A^{(3)}_{p,\theta}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}p&\overline{\theta}{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}&\theta{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}&3-p\\
\theta{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}&2-p&\theta^{2}(1-p)&-\theta{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}\\
\overline{\theta}{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}&\overline{\theta}^{2}(1-p)&2-p&-\overline{\theta}{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}\\
3-p&-\overline{\theta}{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}&-\theta{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}&p\end{pmatrix}

If we take the limits
p\to 1
or
2
, these graphs converges to
y=0
cases above.
Hence we may include them as
p\in[1,2]
.

Those graphs
\{A^{(d)}_{p,\theta}\mid\theta\in\mathbb{T}\}
arising from the sign
\theta
are mutually isomorphic via inner automorphism of
M_{2}
by
u_{\theta}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&\theta\end{pmatrix}
:

Lemma 3.2
.

It follows that

\displaystyle A^{(d)}_{p,\theta}=\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(u_{\theta}^{*})A^{(d)}_{p,1}\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(u_{\theta}).

(3.13)

Proof.

The adjoint action

\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(u_{\theta})\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}=u_{\theta}\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}u_{\theta}^{*}=\begin{pmatrix}a&\overline{\theta}b\\
\theta c&d\end{pmatrix}

has a diagonal unitary matrix expression

\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(u_{\theta})=\begin{pmatrix}1&&&0\\
&\overline{\theta}&&\\
&&\theta&\\
0&&&1\end{pmatrix}

with respect to the ONB

(\widetilde{e_{11}},\widetilde{e_{12}},\widetilde{e_{21}},\widetilde{e_{22}})
.
Hence

\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(u_{\theta}^{*})A\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(u_{\theta})=\begin{pmatrix}1&&&0\\
&\theta&&\\
&&\overline{\theta}&\\
0&&&1\end{pmatrix}A\begin{pmatrix}1&&&0\\
&\overline{\theta}&&\\
&&\theta&\\
0&&&1\end{pmatrix}

is the entrywise product of
A
and

\begin{pmatrix}1&\overline{\theta}&\theta&1\\
\theta&1&\theta^{2}&\theta\\
\overline{\theta}&\overline{\theta}^{2}&1&\overline{\theta}\\
1&\overline{\theta}&\theta&1\end{pmatrix}.

Therefore we have

A^{(d)}_{p,\theta}=\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(u_{\theta}^{*})A^{(d)}_{p,1}\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(u_{\theta})
.
∎

Moreover those graphs
\{A^{(d)}_{p,1}\mid p\in[1,2]\}
are also mutually isomorphic via inner automorphism of
M_{2}
by

\displaystyle v_{p}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{1+\sqrt{2-p}}&\sqrt{1-\sqrt{2-p}}\\
-\sqrt{1-\sqrt{2-p}}&\sqrt{1+\sqrt{2-p}}\end{pmatrix}
:

Lemma 3.3
.

It follows that

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(v_{p}^{*})A^{(3)}_{p,1}\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(v_{p})=A^{(3)}_{1,1}\quad\textrm{ and }\quad\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(v_{p})A^{(2)}_{3-p,1}\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(v_{p}^{*})=A^{(2)}_{2,1}.

(3.14)

Proof.

\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(v_{p})
has a unitary matrix expression

\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(v_{p})=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1+\sqrt{2-p}&\sqrt{p-1}&\sqrt{p-1}&1-\sqrt{2-p}\\
-\sqrt{p-1}&1+\sqrt{2-p}&-(1-\sqrt{2-p})&\sqrt{p-1}\\
-\sqrt{p-1}&-(1-\sqrt{2-p})&1+\sqrt{2-p}&\sqrt{p-1}\\
1-\sqrt{2-p}&-\sqrt{p-1}&-\sqrt{p-1}&1+\sqrt{2-p}\end{pmatrix}

with respect to the ONB

(\widetilde{e_{11}},\widetilde{e_{12}},\widetilde{e_{21}},\widetilde{e_{22}})
,
and we can directly compute (
3.14
).

Abstractly
v_{p}
is a unitary matrix such that
\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(v_{p})
maps the eigenvector

\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}

for the eigenvalue
-1
of
A^{(3)}_{1,1}
to the eigenvector

\begin{pmatrix}-\sqrt{2-p}&\sqrt{p-1}\\
\sqrt{p-1}&\sqrt{2-p}\end{pmatrix}

for the eigenvalue
-1
of
A^{(3)}_{p,1}
.
Since
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits(A^{(3)}_{p,1})=\{3,1,1,-1\}
and
\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(v_{p})
also preserves the eigenvector
1_{M_{2}}
for the eigenvalue
3
, the orthogonality of eigenspaces implies

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(v_{p}^{*})A^{(3)}_{p,1}\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(v_{p})=A^{(3)}_{1,1}.

(3.15)

By the correspondence between a quantum graph
A
and its reflexive complement
as in Proposition
2.28

A^{c}\coloneqq\mathrm{id}_{B}+{\left\lvert{B}\right\rvert}\tau(\cdot)1_{B}-A,

we do not need the latter equality in (
3.14
) for the proof of Theorem
3.1
because the complement preserves isomorphism classes of quantum graphs, and the graphs of degree
2
and
3
are mutual complements.
But here we show (
3.14
) explicitly. In this case

\displaystyle(A^{(d)}_{p,\theta})^{c}=\mathrm{id}+4\tau(\cdot)1-A^{(d)}_{p,\theta}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&&&0\\
&1&&\\
&&1&\\
0&&&1\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}2&&&2\\
&0&&\\
&&0&\\
2&&&2\end{pmatrix}-\begin{pmatrix}p&\overline{\theta}{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}&\theta{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}&d-p\\
\theta{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}&2-p&\theta^{2}(4-d-p)&-\theta{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}\\
\overline{\theta}{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}&\overline{\theta}^{2}(4-d-p)&2-p&-\overline{\theta}{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}\\
d-p&-\overline{\theta}{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}&-\theta{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}&p\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}3-p&-\overline{\theta}{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}&-\theta{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}&2-d+p\\
-\theta{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}&p-1&-\theta^{2}(4-d-p)&\theta{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}\\
-\overline{\theta}{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}&-\overline{\theta}^{2}(4-d-p)&p-1&\overline{\theta}{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}\\
2-d+p&\overline{\theta}{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}&\theta{\left\lvert{y}\right\rvert}&3-p\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=A^{(5-d)}_{3-p,-\theta},

(3.16)

where the last equality follows from
p-1=2-(3-p)
,
2-d+p=(5-d)-(3-p)
, and
-(4-d-p)=4-(5-d)-(3-p)
.
Note that the complement and the conjugation by
\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(u)
for unitary
u\in B
commute as

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(u^{*})A^{c}\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(u)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(u^{*})\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(u)+{\left\lvert{B}\right\rvert}\tau(u\cdot u^{*})u^{*}1u-\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(u^{*})A\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(u)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{id}+{\left\lvert{B}\right\rvert}\tau(\cdot)1-\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(u^{*})A\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(u)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(u^{*})A\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(u)\right)^{c},

(3.17)

thereby

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(v_{p})A^{(2)}_{3-p,1}\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(v_{p}^{*})

\displaystyle\overset{\textrm{(\ref{M2-c})}}{=}\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(v_{p})\left(A^{(3)}_{p,-1}\right)^{c}\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(v_{p}^{*})

\displaystyle\overset{\textrm{(\ref{M2-ad/c})}}{=}\left(\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(v_{p})A^{(3)}_{p,-1}\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(v_{p}^{*})\right)^{c}

\displaystyle\overset{\textrm{(\ref{M2-utheta})}}{=}\left(\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(v_{p}u_{-1}^{*})A^{(3)}_{p,1}\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(u_{-1}v_{p}^{*})\right)^{c}

and since
u_{-1}v_{p}^{*}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{1+\sqrt{2-p}}&-\sqrt{1-\sqrt{2-p}}\\
-\sqrt{1-\sqrt{2-p}}&-\sqrt{1+\sqrt{2-p}}\end{pmatrix}=v_{p}u_{-1}
,

\displaystyle\overset{\phantom{(5.15)}}{=}\left(\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(u_{-1}^{*}v_{p}^{*})A^{(3)}_{p,1}\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(v_{p}u_{-1})\right)^{c}

\displaystyle\overset{\textrm{(\ref{M2-vp3})}}{=}\left(\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(u_{-1}^{*})A^{(3)}_{1,1}\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits(u_{-1})\right)^{c}

\displaystyle\overset{\textrm{(\ref{M2-utheta})}}{=}\left(A^{(3)}_{1,-1}\right)^{c}\overset{\textrm{(\ref{M2-c})}}{=}A^{(2)}_{2,1}.

∎

Therefore up to inner automorphism, there is a unique reflexive quantum graph on
M_{2}
for every degree
d\in\{1,2,3,4\}
.
Since an inner automorphism is a classical isomorphism (1-dimensional quantum isomorphism) and
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits(G)
is invariant under quantum isomorphism,
the complete system of representatives for the classical and quantum isomorphism classes of quantum graphs on
M_{2}
is given by the following.

\begin{array}[]{|c|c|c|c|c|}p&x&z=y&d=p+x&\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits(G)=\{p\pm x,2-p\pm{\left\lvert{z}\right\rvert}\}\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr 1&0&0&1&\{1,1,1,1\}\\
\hline\cr 2&0&0&2&\{2,2,0,0\}\\
\hline\cr 1&2&0&3&\{3,-1,1,1\}\\
\hline\cr 2&2&0&4&\{4,0,0,0\}\end{array}

Recall that
G
above are of the form

G=\begin{pmatrix}p&\overline{y}&y&x\\
y&2-p&z&-y\\
\overline{y}&\overline{z}&2-p&-\overline{y}\\
x&-\overline{y}&-y&p\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}p&0&0&x\\
0&2-p&0&0\\
0&0&2-p&0\\
x&0&0&p\end{pmatrix},

therefore the table indicates the quantum graphs in the statement.
∎

Therefore reflexive quantum graph on
M_{2}
can be
d
-regular for
d\in\{1,2,3,4\}
.
Hence irreflexive quantum graph on
M_{2}
can be
d
-regular for
d\in\{0,1,2,3\}
.

3.3
Nontracial quantum graphs on
M_{2}

By unitary diagonalization, a faithful state on
M_{2}
is a unitary conjugate of one of the Powers states
\omega_{q}=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits(Q\,\cdot)

where
q\in(0,1]
and

\displaystyle Q=\frac{1}{1+q^{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&q^{2}\end{pmatrix}
.

Note that
\omega_{1}=\tau_{M_{2}}
, hence we may assume
q\in(0,1)
.

Lemma 3.4
.

The Powers state
\omega_{q}
is a
\delta=q+q^{-1}
-form on
M_{2}
.
Hence
(M_{2},\omega_{q})
is a quantum set.

Proof.

It follows from Proposition
2.8
that

\delta^{2}=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\nolimits(Q^{-1})=(1+q^{2})(1+q^{-2})=(q+q^{-1})^{2}.

∎

Note that we have
Q=\begin{pmatrix}q\delta&0\\
0&q^{-1}\delta\end{pmatrix}^{-1}
.

Let
e_{ij}
be the
(i,j)
matrix unit in
M_{2}
.
By Lemma
2.5
,

\{\widetilde{e_{ij}}\coloneqq e_{ij}Q^{-1/2}\}_{ij}
forms an ONB
for
L^{2}(M_{2},\omega_{q})
. Explicitly these are

\displaystyle\widetilde{e_{11}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{1+q^{2}}e_{11}=\sqrt{q\delta}e_{11};

\displaystyle\widetilde{e_{12}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{1+q^{-2}}e_{12}=\sqrt{q^{-1}\delta}e_{12};

\displaystyle\widetilde{e_{21}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{1+q^{2}}e_{21}=\sqrt{q\delta}e_{21};

\displaystyle\widetilde{e_{22}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{1+q^{-2}}e_{22}=\sqrt{q^{-1}\delta}e_{22}.

Then we have

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=(q\delta)^{-1/2}\widetilde{e_{11}}+(q^{-1}\delta)^{-1/2}\widetilde{e_{22}};

\displaystyle m(\widetilde{e_{ij}}\otimes\widetilde{e_{kl}})

\displaystyle=Q^{-1/2}_{jk}\widetilde{e_{il}};

\displaystyle m^{\dagger}\widetilde{e_{ij}}

\displaystyle=(q\delta)^{1/2}\widetilde{e_{i1}}\otimes\widetilde{e_{1j}}+(q^{-1}\delta)^{1/2}\widetilde{e_{i2}}\otimes\widetilde{e_{2j}};

\displaystyle\omega_{q}(\widetilde{e_{ij}})

\displaystyle=Q^{1/2}_{ij}.

For a quantum graph
(M_{2},\omega_{q},A)
, we put

A_{ij}^{kl}=\braket{\widetilde{e_{kl}}|A\widetilde{e_{ij}}}
and

A=\begin{pmatrix}A^{11}_{11}&A^{11}_{12}&A^{11}_{21}&A^{11}_{22}\\
A^{12}_{11}&A^{12}_{12}&A^{12}_{21}&A^{12}_{22}\\
A^{21}_{11}&A^{21}_{12}&A^{21}_{21}&A^{21}_{22}\\
A^{22}_{11}&A^{22}_{12}&A^{22}_{21}&A^{22}_{22}\end{pmatrix}.

Rewriting the diagramatic definitions as equations of the coefficients,
this operator
A
is:

a)

self-adjoint if and only if
A_{ij}^{kl}=\overline{A_{kl}^{ij}}
.

b)

real if and only if
A_{ij}^{kl}=\overline{A_{ji}^{lk}}Q_{ii}^{1/2}Q_{jj}^{-1/2}Q_{kk}^{-1/2}Q_{ll}^{1/2}
.

c)

Schur idempotent if and only if
\delta^{2}A_{ij}^{kl}=\sum_{u,v}Q_{uu}^{-1/2}Q_{vv}^{-1/2}A_{iu}^{kv}A_{uj}^{vl}

Schur idempotent
=q\delta A_{i1}^{k1}A_{1j}^{1l}+\delta A_{i2}^{k1}A_{2j}^{1l}+\delta A_{i1}^{k2}A_{1j}^{2l}+q^{-1}\delta A_{i2}^{k2}A_{2j}^{2l}
.

d)

reflexive if and only if
\delta^{2}\delta_{i}^{k}=\sum_{u}Q_{uu}^{-1}A_{iu}^{ku}=q\delta A_{i1}^{k1}+q^{-1}\delta A_{i2}^{k2}
.

Theorem 3.5
.

An undirected reflexive quantum graph
(M_{2},\omega_{q},A)
with
q\in(0,1),\delta=q+q^{-1}
is exactly one of the following.

1)

The trivial quantum graph
A_{1}=\mathrm{id}_{B}=\begin{pmatrix}1&&&0\\
&1&&\\
&&1&\\
0&&&1\end{pmatrix}
, which is
1
-regular with

\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits(A)=\{1,1,1,1\}
.

2)

A_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}q^{-1}\delta&&&\\
&0&&\\
&&0&\\
&&&q\delta\end{pmatrix}
, which is irregular with

\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits(A)=\{q^{-1}\delta,q\delta,0,0\}
.

3)

A_{3}=\begin{pmatrix}1&&&\delta\\
&1&&\\
&&1&\\
\delta&&&1\end{pmatrix}
, which is irregular with

\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits(A)=\{1+\delta,1,1,1-\delta\}
.

4)

The complete quantum graph
A_{4}=\delta^{2}\omega_{q}(\cdot)1=\begin{pmatrix}q^{-1}\delta&&&\delta\\
&0&&\\
&&0&\\
\delta&&&q\delta\end{pmatrix}
,
which is
\delta^{2}
-regular with

\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits(A)=\{\delta^{2},0,0,0\}
.

Proof.

By (a) and (b) we put

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle=A_{11}^{11}\overset{(\text{a})}{=}\overline{A_{11}^{11}};\quad t=A_{12}^{12}\overset{(\text{a})}{=}\overline{A_{12}^{12}}\overset{(\text{b})}{=}A_{21}^{21};

\displaystyle p^{\prime}

\displaystyle=A_{22}^{22}\overset{(\text{a})}{=}\overline{A_{22}^{22}};\quad x=A_{22}^{11}\overset{(\text{b})}{=}\overline{A_{22}^{11}}\overset{(\text{a})}{=}{A_{11}^{22}};

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=A_{12}^{11}\overset{(\text{a})}{=}\overline{A_{11}^{12}}\overset{(\text{b})}{=}qA_{11}^{21}\overset{(\text{a})}{=}q\overline{A_{21}^{11}}\overset{(\text{b})}{=}q^{2}A_{12}^{11};

\displaystyle y^{\prime}

\displaystyle=A_{21}^{22}\overset{(\text{a})}{=}\overline{A_{22}^{21}}\overset{(\text{b})}{=}q^{-1}A_{22}^{12}\overset{(\text{a})}{=}q^{-1}\overline{A_{12}^{22}}\overset{(\text{b})}{=}q^{-2}A_{21}^{22};

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=A_{12}^{21}\overset{(\text{a})}{=}\overline{A_{21}^{12}}\overset{(\text{b})}{=}q^{2}A_{12}^{21}.

Then
y=q^{2}y,y^{\prime}=q^{-2}y^{\prime},z=q^{2}z
and
0<q<1
imply
y=y^{\prime}=z=0
.
Thus

A=\begin{pmatrix}A^{11}_{11}&A^{11}_{12}&A^{11}_{21}&A^{11}_{22}\\
A^{12}_{11}&A^{12}_{12}&A^{12}_{21}&A^{12}_{22}\\
A^{21}_{11}&A^{21}_{12}&A^{21}_{21}&A^{21}_{22}\\
A^{22}_{11}&A^{22}_{12}&A^{22}_{21}&A^{22}_{22}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}p&0&0&x\\
0&t&0&0\\
0&0&t&0\\
x&0&0&p^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}

where
p,t,p^{\prime},x\in\mathbb{R}
.
By (d):
\delta\delta_{i}^{k}=qA_{i1}^{k1}+q^{-1}A_{i2}^{k2}
,
we have

\displaystyle\delta=qp+q^{-1}t;\quad\delta=qt+q^{-1}p^{\prime}.

(3.18)

By (c
{}_{ij}^{kl}
):

\delta A_{ij}^{kl}=qA_{i1}^{k1}A_{1j}^{1l}+A_{i2}^{k1}A_{2j}^{1l}+A_{i1}^{k2}A_{1j}^{2l}+q^{-1}A_{i2}^{k2}A_{2j}^{2l}
,
we obtain

\displaystyle(\text{c}_{11}^{11})

\displaystyle\quad\delta p=qp^{2}+q^{-1}t^{2}

\displaystyle(\text{c}_{12}^{12})

\displaystyle\quad\delta t=qpt+q^{-1}tp^{\prime}

\displaystyle(\text{c}_{22}^{22})

\displaystyle\quad\delta p^{\prime}=qt^{2}+q^{-1}{p^{\prime}}^{2}

\displaystyle(\text{c}_{22}^{11})

\displaystyle\quad\delta x=x^{2}

Substituting (
3.18
) for
t
in (c
{}_{11}^{11}
),

\displaystyle\delta p=qp^{2}+q(\delta-qp)^{2}=q(p^{2}+\delta^{2}-2q\delta p+q^{2}p^{2})

\displaystyle\delta^{2}-(q^{-1}+2q)\delta p+(1+q^{2})p^{2}=0.

Since
1+q^{2}=q\delta
, division by
\delta
deduces

\delta-(\delta+q)p+qp^{2}=(\delta-qp)(1-p)=0.

Thus (
3.18
) implies

\displaystyle(p,t,p^{\prime})=(1,1,1),(q^{-1}\delta,0,q\delta).

(3.19)

These solutions also satisfy (c
{}_{12}^{12}
) and (c
{}_{22}^{22}
).
Independently (c
{}_{22}^{11}
) shows
x=0,\delta
.
Therefore undirected quantum graphs are the four graphs in the statement:

(p,t,p^{\prime},x)=A_{1}(1,1,1,0),\ A_{2}(q^{-1}\delta,0,q\delta,0),\ A_{3}(1,1,1,\delta),\ A_{4}(q^{-1}\delta,0,q\delta,\delta).

Now
A
is
d
-regular if and only if

(q\delta)^{1/2}1_{M_{2}}=q\widetilde{e_{11}}+\widetilde{e_{22}}
is an eigenvector of eigenvalue
d
for
A
:

A\begin{pmatrix}q\\
0\\
0\\
1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}pq+x\\
0\\
0\\
xq+p^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}=d\begin{pmatrix}q\\
0\\
0\\
1\end{pmatrix},

i.e.,
d=p+q^{-1}x=xq+p^{\prime}
. Thus
A_{1}
is
1
-regular,
A_{4}
is
\delta^{2}
-regular, and
A_{2},A_{3}
are irregular.
∎

4
Quantum automorphism groups of quantum graphs on
M_{2}

4.1
Quantum automorphism groups of tracial
(M_{2},\tau,A)

For each
d=1,2,3,4
, let
\mathcal{G}_{d}=(M_{2},\tau,A_{d})
be the
d
-regular quantum graph as in Theorem
3.1
.

Theorem 4.1
.

The quantum automorphism group of the
1
-regular (trivial) or
4
-regular (complete) quantum graphs on
(M_{2},\tau)
is the special orthogonal group
SO(3)
:

\mathop{\mathrm{Qut}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G}_{1})=\mathop{\mathrm{Qut}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G}_{4})\cong SO(3).

Proof.

Since the quantum automorphism group of trivial and complete graphs are the quantum symmetry group
\mathop{\mathrm{Qut}}\nolimits(M_{2},\tau)=(A_{aut}(M_{2}),\Delta)
defined by Wang
[
18
, Proposition 4.2]
, the statement follows directly from the computation by Sołtan
[
16
, Theorem 5.2]
.

Concretely
[
16
, Theorem 5.2]
shows

\displaystyle C(\mathop{\mathrm{Qut}}\nolimits(M_{2},\tau))\cong C(SO(3))

\displaystyle=C^{*}\left\langle S,T,R\text{ normal, commuting}\middle|ST=-R^{2},{\left\lvert{S}\right\rvert}+{\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert}=1\right\rangle

with compatible comultiplication. This is the universal coefficient algebra with fundamental representation with respect to
(\widetilde{e_{11}},\widetilde{e_{12}},\widetilde{e_{21}},\widetilde{e_{22}})
:

u=(u_{ij}^{kl})=\begin{pmatrix}1-K&-R&-R^{*}&K\\
C&S&T^{*}&-C\\
C^{*}&T&S^{*}&-C^{*}\\
K&R&R^{*}&1-K\end{pmatrix}

where
K=R^{*}R+T^{*}T,C=SR^{*}-RT
. The generators
S,T,R
correspond to the coordinate functions
s,t,r
of
SO(3)
subgroup of
SU(3)
as follows:

v^{*}SO(3)v=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}s&\overline{t}&\sqrt{2}(r\overline{t}-s\overline{r})\\
t&\overline{s}&\sqrt{2}(t\overline{r}-r\overline{s})\\
\sqrt{2}r&\sqrt{2}\overline{r}&{\left\lvert{s}\right\rvert}^{2}-{\left\lvert{t}\right\rvert}^{2}\end{pmatrix}\middle|\begin{matrix}st=-r^{2}\\
{\left\lvert{s}\right\rvert}+{\left\lvert{t}\right\rvert}=1\end{matrix}\right\}\subset SU(3)

where
\displaystyle v=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&1&0\\
-i&i&0\\
0&0&\sqrt{2}\end{pmatrix}\in U(3)
and

v\begin{pmatrix}s&\overline{t}&\sqrt{2}(r\overline{t}-s\overline{r})\\
t&\overline{s}&\sqrt{2}(t\overline{r}-r\overline{s})\\
\sqrt{2}r&\sqrt{2}\overline{r}&{\left\lvert{s}\right\rvert}^{2}-{\left\lvert{t}\right\rvert}^{2}\end{pmatrix}v^{*}=\begin{pmatrix}\Re(s+t)&-\Im(s+t)&2\Re(r\overline{t}-s\overline{r})\\
\Im(s-t)&\Re(s-t)&2\Im(r\overline{t}-s\overline{r})\\
2\Re r&2\Im{r}&{\left\lvert{s}\right\rvert}^{2}-{\left\lvert{t}\right\rvert}^{2}\end{pmatrix}.

Since
A_{1}=\mathrm{id},A_{4}=4\tau(\cdot)1
commute with
u
, we have

\mathop{\mathrm{Qut}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G}_{1})=\mathop{\mathrm{Qut}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G}_{4})=SO(3)
.
∎

Theorem 4.2
.

The quantum automorphism group of the
2
-regular or
3
-regular quantum graphs on
(M_{2},\tau)
is a subgroup of
SO(3)
that is isomorphic to the orthogonal group
O(2)
:

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Qut}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G}_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{Qut}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G}_{3})\cong O(2)

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{G}_{3}=\mathcal{G}_{2}^{c}
, we have
\mathop{\mathrm{Qut}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G}_{2})=\mathop{\mathrm{Qut}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G}_{3})
.
It suffices to compute
\mathchoice{\text{\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{$C(SO(3))$}\!\Big/\!\lower 4.30554pt\hbox{$\langle A_{2}u=uA_{2}\rangle$}}}{{C(SO(3))}\,/\,{\langle A_{2}u=uA_{2}\rangle}}{{C(SO(3))}\,/\,{\langle A_{2}u=uA_{2}\rangle}}{{C(SO(3))}\,/\,{\langle A_{2}u=uA_{2}\rangle}}
.
Then
A_{2}u=uA_{2}
implies

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}1-K&-R&-R^{*}&K\\
0&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&0\\
K&R&R^{*}&1-K\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1-K&0&0&K\\
C&0&0&-C\\
C^{*}&0&0&-C^{*}\\
K&0&0&1-K\end{pmatrix},

hence
C=R=0
.
Since
C=SR^{*}-RT
, the additional relation is
R=0
.
Then
ST=-R^{2}=0
, and

{\left\lvert{S}\right\rvert}+{\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert}=1
implies
{\left\lvert{S}\right\rvert}=1
or
{\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert}=1
.
Therefore it follows that

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Qut}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G}_{2})

\displaystyle\cong\left\{\begin{pmatrix}t&0&0\\
0&\overline{t}&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0&\overline{t}&0\\
t&0&0\\
0&0&-1\end{pmatrix}\middle|{\left\lvert{t}\right\rvert}=1\right\}

\displaystyle\overset{(\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits v)}{\cong}\left\{\begin{pmatrix}x&0\\
0&\det x\end{pmatrix}\middle|x\in O(2)\right\}\cong O(2).

where
v
is as in the proof of Theorem
4.1
.
∎

4.2
Quantum automorphism groups of nontracial
(M_{2},\omega_{q},A)

For each
d=1,2,3,4
, let
\mathcal{G}_{d}
be the quantum graph on
(M_{2},\omega_{q})
for
q\in(0,1)
with adjacency operator
A_{d}
as in Theorem
3.5
.

Theorem 4.3
.

The quantum automorphism groups of the trivial and complete graphs
\mathcal{G}_{1},\mathcal{G}_{4}
are the quantum special orthogonal group
SO_{q}(3)
:

\mathop{\mathrm{Qut}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G}_{1})=\mathop{\mathrm{Qut}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G}_{4})\cong SO_{q}(3).

Proof.

Similarly to Theorem
4.1
, the quantum automorphism group of trivial and complete graphs are the quantum symmetry group
\mathop{\mathrm{Qut}}\nolimits(M_{2},\omega_{q})=(A_{aut}^{Q}(M_{2}),\Delta)
defined by Wang
[
18
, Proposition 4.2]
, and hence the statement follows from the computation by Sołtan
[
16
, Theorem 4.3]
.

Concretely
[
16
, Theorem 4.3]
shows that

\displaystyle C(\mathop{\mathrm{Qut}}\nolimits(M_{2},\omega_{q}))\cong C(SO_{q}(3))=C^{*}\left\langle A,G,L\right\rangle

is generated by the universal coefficients of the fundamental representation with respect to
(\widetilde{e_{11}},\widetilde{e_{12}},\widetilde{e_{21}},\widetilde{e_{22}})
:

u=(u_{ij}^{kl})=\begin{pmatrix}1-q^{2}K&-A&-qA^{*}&qK\\
qC&L&-q^{2}G^{*}&-C\\
C^{*}&-G&L^{*}&-q^{-1}C^{*}\\
qK&q^{-1}A&A^{*}&1-K\end{pmatrix}

where
K=A^{*}A+G^{*}G,C=q^{-1}LA^{*}+q^{2}AG^{*}
. By Podleś
[
15
, Proposition 3.1]
, their defining relations are the following:

\displaystyle L^{*}L

\displaystyle=(1-K)(1-q^{-2}K)

\displaystyle LL^{*}

\displaystyle=(1-q^{2}K)(1-q^{4}K)

\displaystyle G^{*}G

\displaystyle=GG^{*}=K^{2}

\displaystyle A^{*}A

\displaystyle=C^{*}C=K-K^{2}

\displaystyle AA^{*}

\displaystyle=CC^{*}=q^{2}K-q^{4}K^{2}

\displaystyle A^{2}

\displaystyle=q^{-1}LG

\displaystyle LG

\displaystyle=q^{4}GL

\displaystyle LA

\displaystyle=q^{2}AL

\displaystyle AG

\displaystyle=q^{2}GA

\displaystyle LG^{*}

\displaystyle=q^{4}G^{*}L

\displaystyle A^{*}L

\displaystyle=q^{-1}(1-K)C

\displaystyle LK

\displaystyle=q^{4}KL

\displaystyle GK

\displaystyle=KG

\displaystyle AK

\displaystyle=q^{2}KA

\displaystyle CK

\displaystyle=q^{2}KC

\displaystyle AC

\displaystyle=CA

Since
A_{1}=\mathrm{id},A_{4}=\delta^{2}\omega_{q}(\cdot)1
commute with
u
, we have

\mathop{\mathrm{Qut}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G}_{1})=\mathop{\mathrm{Qut}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G}_{4})=SO_{q}(3)
.
∎

Theorem 4.4
.

The quantum automorphism groups of
\mathcal{G}_{2},\mathcal{G}_{3}
are the torus subgroup
\mathbb{T}
of
SO_{q}(3)
:

\mathop{\mathrm{Qut}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G}_{2})=\mathop{\mathrm{Qut}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G}_{3})\cong\mathbb{T}<SO_{q}(3).

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{G}_{3}=\mathcal{G}_{2}^{c}
, we have
\mathop{\mathrm{Qut}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G}_{2})=\mathop{\mathrm{Qut}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G}_{3})
.
It suffices to compute
\mathchoice{\text{\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{$C(SO(3))$}\!\Big/\!\lower 4.30554pt\hbox{$\langle A_{2}u=uA_{2}\rangle$}}}{{C(SO(3))}\,/\,{\langle A_{2}u=uA_{2}\rangle}}{{C(SO(3))}\,/\,{\langle A_{2}u=uA_{2}\rangle}}{{C(SO(3))}\,/\,{\langle A_{2}u=uA_{2}\rangle}}
.
Then
A_{2}u=uA_{2}
implies

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}q^{-1}(1-q^{2}K)&-q^{-1}A&-A^{*}&K\\
0&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&0\\
q^{2}K&A&qA^{*}&q(1-K)\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}q^{-1}(1-q^{2}K)&0&0&q^{2}K\\
C&0&0&-qC\\
q^{-1}C^{*}&0&0&-C^{*}\\
K&0&0&q(1-K)\end{pmatrix},

hence
A=C=0,K=q^{2}K
. Then
K=0
by
0<q<1
, and
G*G=K-A^{*}A=0
implies
G=0
.
Therefore we have

C(\mathop{\mathrm{Qut}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G}_{2}))=C^{*}\left\langle L\middle|L^{*}L=LL^{*}=1\right\rangle\cong C(\mathbb{T})

where the last isomorphism is via
L\mapsto z=(\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{T}}:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{C})
.
Note that the comultiplication
\Delta
of
\mathop{\mathrm{Qut}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G}_{2})
is now characterized by

\Delta(L)=-qC\otimes A+L\otimes L+G\otimes q^{2}G^{*}-q^{-1}A\otimes C=L\otimes L,

which is isomorphic to the unitary torus

\mathbb{T}=(C(\mathbb{T}),\Delta:z\mapsto z\otimes z)
.
Therefore
\mathop{\mathrm{Qut}}\nolimits(\mathcal{G}_{2})=\mathbb{T}
.
∎

4.3
Quantum isomorphisms between quantum graphs on
M_{2}
and
\mathbb{C}^{4}

\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{d}=(\mathbb{C}^{4},\tau_{\mathbb{C}^{4}},A^{\prime}_{d})

d=1,2,3,4
as in Table
4.1
.
